6.22
)
(blue line). For comparison, the violet line is the case
\beta=\text{const}.=\beta_{1}
.
Used parameters:
\beta_{1}=0.0064
(air),
\beta_{2}=0.3859
(water),

\theta_{0}=\pi/40\text{ rad}
,
\Lambda=0.6\text{ m}
,
g=9.8\text{ m/$\text{s}^{2}$}
.

We also include the graphs of the angular frequency
\dot{\theta}

(which shows corner points) and of the angular acceleration
\ddot{\theta}

(which shows “jumps”, i.e. infinite derivatives at singular times,
as we can directly see from (
6.22
) and (
6.23
)).

Figure 6.6.
First derivative
\dot{\theta}

of the solution of (
6.22
) (blue line). The case
with
\beta=\text{const}=\beta_{1}
is also shown for comparison
(violet line). Note the corner points at the singular moments, for
example at
t=0.083\text{ s}
(scaled in the right figure).

Figure 6.7.
Second derivative
\ddot{\theta}
of the solution of (
6.22
)
(blue line). The case with
\beta=\text{const}=\beta_{1}
is also
shown for comparison (violet line). Note the “jumps”
at the singular moments, for example at
t=0.083\text{ s}
(scaled
in the right figure). The infinitesimal oscillations are caused by
the embedding as GSF of the Heaviside function.

6.3.
Non linear strain-stress model

In this section, we want to show how to construct a mathematical model
starting from an empirical function (the strain-stress relation for
a steel sample) and representing it as a GSF. Starting from Newton’s
second law, we hence arrive at a single nonlinear equation describing
the behaviour of the steel sample. Since the empirical function is
not differentiable at the end of the linear part, the use of GSF is
therefore essential.

The strain-stress curve we consider is shown in Fig.
6.8
.

Figure 6.8.
Strain-stress empirical model
of the steel (see
[
61
]
).

We recall that stress can be defined as
\sigma=\frac{F}{S_{0}}
,
where
F
is the force applied to the sample, and
S_{0}
is the
initial cross-section of the cylindrical sample. The strain
\varepsilon

is usually introduced as
\varepsilon=\frac{L-L_{0}}{L_{0}}
, where

L_{0}
is the unstressed length and
L
is the length after force
application. In order to reproduce the experimental dependence of
Fig.
6.8
we considered the parameters

d=0.37\,
mm for the diameter of the steel cilinder, and
L_{0}=2.2\,
m
for the unstressed length of the sample. Thus, during the elastic
behaviour (linear part) we have a Young’s modulus
E=\frac{\sigma}{\varepsilon}=2.13\cdot 10^{11}\,\text{Pa}
,
a stiffness
k=\frac{ES_{0}}{L_{0}}=10423\,\frac{\text{N}}{\text{m}}
,
and hence the magnitude of the linear part of the force is given by

F_{\text{l}}(x)=kx
. For the nonlinear part of the empirical law,
we use the Mathematica built-in function NonlinearModelFit, see
[
80
]
.
The result is shown in Fig.
6.9
.

Figure 6.9.
Non-linear part modelling of
the strain-stress curve using the NonlinearModelFit. The dots are
values extracted from the original data.

The resulting expression is

F_{\text{n}}(x)=a_{1}\exp(a_{2}x)+a_{3}\cos(a_{4}x)+a_{5}x+a_{6}x^{2}+a_{7}x^{3}+a_{8}x^{4}+a_{9}x^{5}+a_{10}x^{6}+a_{11}x^{7},

(6.24)

where the coefficients
a_{k}
are given in Tab.
1
.

a_{1}=1.5\cdot 10^{3}

a_{5}=-9.9\cdot 10^{4}

a_{9}=-1.8\cdot 10^{9}

a_{2}=3.9

a_{6}=2.8\cdot 10^{6}

a_{10}=4.8\cdot 10^{9}

a_{3}=3.0

a_{7}=-4.4\cdot 10^{7}

a_{11}=-5.1\cdot 10^{9}

a_{4}=1.0\cdot 10^{2}

a_{8}=3.8\cdot 10^{8}

Table 1.
Coefficients used in non-linear part
F_{\text{n}}

of the force.

Using the Heaviside function, we can hence write the force
F
acting
on the steel sample as

F(x)=-F_{\text{l}}(x)-(F_{\text{n}}(x)-F_{\text{l}}(x))H(x-x_{0}),

(6.25)

and it is represented in Fig.
6.10
.
Note that the negative sign is due to the fact that the force is directed
opposite to the elongation of the sample; moreover,
x_{0}=0.033

according to Fig.
6.10
. Thus,
the position
x
of the steel sample satisfies the differential equation

\ddot{x}-\frac{F(x)}{m}=0.

(6.26)

Once again, note that the GSF
F
is nonlinear and the term
F(x(t))

is a composition of GSF.

Figure 6.10.
Recomputed strain-stress
model.

Far from the singularity
x=x_{0}
, the validity of (
6.26
)
can also be seen using the conservation of the mechanical energy.
In fact, if
x
is far from
x_{0}
, in the sense that
x\leq x_{1}

for some
x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}_{<0}
, then
F(x)=-F_{\text{l}}(x)
and we
are in the zone of Hooke’s law; we thus have the potential energy:

U(x)=\frac{kx^{2}}{2}
. Similarly, if
x\geq x_{2}
for some
x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
then
F(x)=-F_{\text{n}}(x)
and
U(x)=\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}\exp(a_{2}x)+\frac{a_{3}}{a_{4}}\sin(a_{4}x)+\frac{a_{5}}{2}x^{2}+\frac{a_{6}}{3}x^{3}+\frac{a_{7}}{4}x^{4}+\frac{a_{8}}{5}x^{5}+\frac{a_{9}}{6}x^{6}+\frac{a_{10}}{7}x^{7}+\frac{a_{11}}{8}x^{8}
.
Therefore, far from the singularity, the conservation of the mechanical
energy is equivalent to (
6.26
).

Clearly, the nonlinear behaviour depends on the initial conditions:
if
x(0)=0.0\,\text{m}
and
\dot{x}(0)=5\,\frac{\text{m}}{\text{s}}
,
we remain in the linear setting, whereas for
x(0)=0.0\,\text{m}
,

\dot{x}(0)=15\,\frac{\text{m}}{\text{s}}
we enter into the nonlinear
one, see Fig.
6.11
.

Figure 6.11.
The solution
x(t)
of equation
6.26

with initial conditions
x(0)=0.0\,\text{m},
\dot{x}(0)=15\,\frac{\text{m}}{\text{s}}

(blue line) in comparison with the solution in the linear setting
for
x(0)=0.0\,\text{m}
,
\dot{x}(0)=5\,\frac{\text{m}}{\text{s}}

(violet line).

See also
[
13
,
63
,
64
,
65
]
for
more complete models of this type in the setting of Colombeau theory.

6.4.
Discontinuous Lagrangians in optics (Snell’s law derivation)

A typical example where one would like to use the usual results of
calculus despite dealing with non differentiable functions, is geometrical
optics at the interface of two media, where usually the Lagrangian
function is not smooth. It is well-know, e.g., that the rigorous derivation
of Snell’s law is a paradigmatic example, see e.g.
[
77
]
. The
main aim of this section is to show the features of the nonlinear
calculus of GSF by deriving Snell’s law for plane stratified media
from the classical Fermat’s principle. For example, in the following
deduction, the refraction index
n(x)
can be any GSF, e.g. the
embedding of a locally integrable function. For more general versions
of Snell’s law in different media, see e.g.
[
12
,
67
]
. For
the classical deduction where the refraction index
n(x)
and the
light path
x=x(s)
are
\mathcal{C}^{2}
functions, see e.g.
[
46
,
60
]
.

We assume that we are considering a body
B:=\langle B_{\varepsilon}\rangle\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}

represented by the strongly internal set generated by the net
B_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
and that our light path satisfies the classical Fermat principle,
i.e. the path of light between two given points
P
,
Q\in B
is
the one which minimizes the travel time. In order to mathematically
state this principle for GSF, we introduce the space of paths (see

[
49
,
23
]
for details):

\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}_{\text{bd}}(P,Q):=\left\{r\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}([0,1],B)\mid r(0)=P,\ r(1)=Q\right\},

(6.27)

and the travel time functional

T[r]:=\frac{1}{c}\int_{0}^{1}n(r)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}r}{\hbox{\rm d}r}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}r$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}r$}}:=\frac{1}{c}\int_{0}^{1}n(r(s))\left|\dot{r}(s)\right|\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}\quad\forall r\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}_{\text{bd}}(P,Q).

(6.28)

As usual,
c
is the speed of light in vacuum, and
n\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(B,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})

is the refraction index of the media
B
we are considering. The
Fermat principle hence implies that the light path
r\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}_{\text{bd}}(P,Q)

is a weak extremal of the travel time functional
T[-]
, i.e. it
satisfies

\delta T(r;h):=\left.\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}}T[r+xh]\right|_{x=0}=0\quad\forall h\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}_{\text{bd}}(0,0).

(6.29)

Note that, since in (
6.27
) we consider only paths
r\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}([0,1],B)

valued in the strongly internal set
B=\langle B_{\varepsilon}\rangle
,
Thm.
11
implies

\forall h\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}_{\text{bd}}(0,0)\,\exists\delta\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\,\forall x\in(-\delta,\delta):\ r+xh\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}([0,1],B),

and therefore, it is correct to consider the derivative in (
6.29
).
Physically this means that we are considering only paths which lay
completely inside the body
B=\langle B_{\varepsilon}\rangle
. The weak
extremal condition (
6.29
) is equivalent to the Euler-Lagrange
equations (see
[
49
]
) for the Lagrangian
(r,v)\mapsto L(r,v):=n(r)\sqrt{v\cdot v}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(B\times\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{3},\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
.
We use the notation
L(r,v)=L(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})

for the variables of
L
. We also explicitly note the nonlinear operations
in this Lagrangian, and the composition
n(r(s))
of GSF. We use
the customary notations
\vec{v}(s):=\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}r}{\hbox{\rm d}r}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}r$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}r$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}}(s)\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
,

v(s):=|\vec{v}(s)|\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, and
L[r](s):=L(r(s),\vec{v}(s))
.
We hence get

\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}}\left(\frac{\partial L}{\partial v_{j}}[r](s)\right)=\frac{\partial L}{\partial r_{j}}[r](s),\quad\forall j=1,2,3,\ \forall s\in[0,1].

(6.30)

We always assume that the frame of reference is chosen so that the
light path satisfies
v(s)\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
. Calculating the derivatives

\partial L/\partial v_{j}
and
\partial L/\partial r_{j}
in Euler-Lagrange
equations, we get

\displaystyle\frac{\partial L}{\partial v_{j}}(r,v)

\displaystyle=n(r)\frac{v_{j}}{\sqrt{v\cdot v}}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial L}{\partial r_{j}}(r,v)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial n}{\partial r_{j}}(r)\sqrt{v\cdot v},

for all
(r,v)\in B\times\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
and all
j=1,2,3
. Substituting
in (
6.30
), we obtain the eikonal equation

\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}}\left(n(r(s))\frac{\vec{v}(s)}{v(s)}\right)=\nabla n(r(s))v(s),\quad\forall s\in[0,1].

(6.31)

We now consider the case of a plane stratified media, i.e. where

n
changes only along one direction
\vec{k}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
,
|\vec{k}|=1
,
so that

\nabla n(r(s))\parallel\vec{k}\quad\forall s\in[0,1].

(6.32)

For simplicity, where it is clear, we omit the evaluation
at
s
. Thus, the cross product of the two vectors
\nabla n(r)

and
\vec{k}
is
\vec{k}\times\nabla n(r)=0=\vec{k}\times\nabla n(r)v
.
Using (
6.31
) we get
\vec{k}\times\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}}(n(r)\frac{\vec{v}}{v})=0
,
and hence
\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}}(\vec{k}\times n(r(s))\frac{\vec{v}(s)}{v(s)})=0

for all
s\in[0,1]
, i.e. the function
s\in[0,1]\mapsto\vec{k}\times n(r(s))\frac{\vec{v}(s)}{v(s)}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{3}

is a constant
\vec{C}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. Taking its magnitude
C=|\vec{C}|

\forall s\in[0,1]:\ C=|\vec{k}|\left|n(r(s))\right|\left|\frac{\vec{v}(s)}{v(s)}\right|\sin\varphi(s),

where
\varphi(s)
is the angle between
\vec{k}
and
\vec{v}(s)=\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}r}{\hbox{\rm d}r}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}r$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}r$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}}(s)
.
We proved Snell’s law for GSF:

Theorem 58
.

Let
B_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
B:=\langle B_{\varepsilon}\rangle
,

P
,
Q\in\langle B_{\varepsilon}\rangle
,
n\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(B,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})
.
Assume that
r\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}_{\text{\emph{bd}}}(P,Q)
is a weak extremal
of the travel time functional (
6.28
), i.e. it satisfies
(
6.29
). Set
\vec{v}(s):=\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}r}{\hbox{\rm d}r}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}r$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}r$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\hbox{\rm d}s}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}s$}}}(s)\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
,

v(s):=|\vec{v}(s)|\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
and assume that
v(s)>0
for all
s\in[0,1]
.
Then the eikonal equation (
6.31
) holds. Moreover, if

\nabla n(r(s))\parallel\vec{k}
, where
\vec{k}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
,
|\vec{k}|=1
,
and
\varphi(s)
is the angle between
\vec{k}
and
\vec{v}(s),
then
the quantity
n(r(s))\cdot\sin\varphi(s)
is constant for all
s\in[0,1]
.

6.5.
Finite and infinite step potential

Models of quantum mechanics such as the potential well or the step
potential with finite or infinite walls are clear and simple examples
showing features of various quantum mechanical effects, see e.g.
[
11
]
.
However, the mathematics of such models is not very clear sometimes,
see again e.g.
[
11
, pag. 34-40, pag. 68]
and authors’ comments
about mathematical rigour. Once again, in this section we see how
the formalism of GSF theory allows one to completely recover a mathematically
and physically clear proof by formalizing the intuitive steps of
[
11
, pag. 68]
.
We consider the step-potential problem, where the high of the potential
can be any finite or infinite generalized number; a similar approach
can be used for the rectangular potential wells.

In the following, we write
x\ll 0
if
\exists r\in\mathbb{R}_{<0}:\ x<r
,
and similarly for
x\gg 0
, and we simply say that
x

is far
from
0
. The step function potential for the one-dimensional stationary
Schrodinger equation is a GSF
U\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}},\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
such that

U(x)=\begin{cases}0&x\ll 0,\\
U_{0}&x\gg 0,\end{cases}

(6.33)

where
U_{0}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
is an arbitrary generalized number (finite
or infinite). For example,
U(x)=H(x)\cdot U_{0}
satisfies these
conditions. However, as stated in
[
11
, pag. 34]
, this is
actually an idealized model of the potential, and we cannot say it
is a physically meaningful model for infinitesimal
x\approx 0
(similarly
to what we have already seen e.g. in Sec.
6.1

for the singular variable length pendulum).

The system satisfies the stationary Schrodinger’s equation

\left[-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}$}}^{2}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}^{2}}+U(x)\right]\psi(x)=E\psi(x),

(6.34)

where
\hbar
is the Planck’s constant,
m\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
the mass of
the particle,
E
the energy, and
\psi(x)
the wave function. Using
Thm.
52
, we can state that there exists a
\psi\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}},\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})

satisfying (
6.34
). Repeating exactly the
usual calculations, for
x\ll 0
we have:

\psi(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{k_{1}}}\left(A_{1}e^{ik_{1}x}+A_{2}e^{-ik_{1}x}\right),

(6.35)

where
k_{1}:=\sqrt{2mE/\hbar^{2}}
and
A_{1}
,
A_{2}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

are undefined constants. For
x\gg 0
, we have:

\psi(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{k_{2}}}\left(B_{1}e^{ik_{2}x}+B_{2}e^{-ik_{2}x}\right),

(6.36)

where
k_{2}=\sqrt{2m(E-V_{0})/\hbar^{2}},

B_{1}
,
B_{2}\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

are undefined constants. As stated in
[
11
, pag. 68]
, in
order to find these constants, we need some mathematically careful
steps to justify the corresponding initial conditions. Take any
standard
real number

\eta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and integrate (
6.34
)
on
[-\eta,\eta]\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
to get

\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}}(\eta)-\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}}(-\eta)=\frac{2m}{\hbar^{2}}\int_{-\eta}^{\eta}\left[U(x)-E\right]\psi(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}.

(6.37)

As in
[
11
, pag. 68]
, we assume that

\displaystyle U(x)-E\text{ is finite for all finite }x\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

(6.38)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}}(\eta)\text{ is finite for all }\eta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}

.

(6.39)

From (
6.37
) and the first of these assumptions, we obtain

\left|\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}}(\eta)-\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}}(-\eta)\right|\leq\frac{4m}{\hbar^{2}}\cdot C\cdot\eta

for some
C\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
(coming from (
6.38
)), i.e.

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\to 0^{+}\\
\eta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\end{subarray}}\left|\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}}(\eta)-\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}}(-\eta)\right|=0.

(6.40)

Similarly, from (
6.39
) and the fundamental theorem of
calculus for GSF Thm.
32
.
(v)
,
we have

\left|\psi(\eta)-\psi(0)\right|=\left|\int_{0}^{\eta}\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}}(x)\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\right|\leq\bar{C}\cdot\eta,

for some
\bar{C}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
(coming from (
6.39
)), and
hence

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\to 0^{+}\\
\eta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\end{subarray}}\left|\psi(\eta)-\psi(0)\right|=0.

(6.41)

Recall that from Thm.
16
.
(ii)

and Thm.
24
it directly follows that both
\psi(x)

and its derivative
\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}}(x)
are GSF, and
hence they are continuous in the sharp topology at each point
x\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
.
Stated explicitly at
x=0
, this means that

\displaystyle\exists\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\to 0\\
x\in\,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}}(0),

\displaystyle\exists\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\to 0\\
x\in\,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}\end{subarray}}\psi(x)

\displaystyle=\psi(0),

and these are different than (
6.40
) and (
6.41
),
where
\eta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Indeed, balls
B_{\eta}(c)\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
,
for radii
\eta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, generate a different topology on
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}

(called
Fermat topology
, see e.g.
[
30
,
31
]
).
See Fig.
6.12
for
an intuitive diagram of the solution
\psi
in an infinitesimal neighborhood
of
x=0
: whereas
\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\hbox{\rm d}\psi}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\psi$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}}(\eta)
is continuous
for
\eta\to 0
,
\eta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, it is well-known (see
[
11
]
)
that the same property does not hold for the second derivative. In
Fig.
6.12
, the green
lines represents the solution for
x\ll 0
or
x\gg 0
, and the blue
one the GSF function
\psi=[\psi_{\varepsilon}(-)]
(we actually represented

\psi_{\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small); we therefore have
to think as infinitesimal the differences between blue and green lines,
and hence as infinite the second derivative at
x=0
.

From (
6.35
), (
6.36
) and (
6.40
),
(
6.41
) we obtain that the constants are uniquely
determined by the system

\begin{cases}\left(A_{1}+A_{2}\right)=\left(B_{1}+B_{2}\right)\\
k_{1}\left(A_{1}-A_{2}\right)=k_{2}\left(B_{1}-B_{2}\right)\end{cases}

(6.42)

Figure 6.12.
The fist derivative
(left) and the second derivative (right) of the wave function
\psi

in an infinitesimal neighborhood of
0
(blue lines). Green lines
are the same derivatives for
x\ll 0
and
x\gg 0
.

6.6.
Heisenberg uncertainty principle

We close this section of applications by mentioning how we can use
infinitesimal and infinite numbers and GSF theory to fully justify
the most frequent example of the Heisenberg uncertainty principle.

We do not have sufficient space here to present a complete list of
result about the so called
hyperfinite Fourier transform
(see

[
59
]
), but we can surely present the main ideas:

(i)

Since in the ring
\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
we have infinite numbers
k\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
,
e.g.
k=\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{-1}
, and since every GSF is always integrable
on a functionally compact set of the form
K:=[-k,k]^{n}\subseteq\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

(see Thm.
46
), we can simply define
the
hyperfinite Fourier transform

\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(f\right)

of any
f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(K,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{C}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{C}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{C}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{C}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{C}})
as

\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(f\right)\left(\omega\right):=\intop_{K}f\left(x\right)e^{-ix\cdotp\omega}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=\intop_{-k}^{k}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x_{1}}{\hbox{\rm d}x_{1}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x_{1}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x_{1}$}}\ldots\intop_{-k}^{k}f\left(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\right)e^{-ix\cdotp\omega}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x_{n}}{\hbox{\rm d}x_{n}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x_{n}$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x_{n}$}}.

(6.43)

(ii)

The main feature of this transform is that, despite the fact that
essentially all the usual classical properties of the Fourier transform
can be proved for
\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(f\right)
, it is well-defined
for
all
GSF
f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(K,\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{C}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{C}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{C}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{C}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{C}})
, even if they are not of
tempered type. Clearly, this allows one to use the Fourier method
to find non-tempered solutions of differential equations. For example,
let
f\left(x\right)=e^{x}
for all
\left|x\right|\leq k
, where

k:=-\log\left(\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}\right)
. The hyperfinite Fourier transform

\mathcal{F}_{k}
of
f
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{k}\left(f\right)\left(\omega\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-i\omega}\left(\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{i\omega}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}}-\frac{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{i\omega}}\right)\quad\forall\omega\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}.

Therefore,
\mathcal{F}_{k}(f)(\omega)
is always an infinite complex
number for all finite numbers
\omega
and hence the non-Archimedean
language is essential here.

(iii)

The set
\text{supp}\left(f\right):=\overline{\left\{x\in X\mid\left|f\left(x\right)\right|>0\right\}}
,
where
\overline{\left(\cdot\right)}
denotes the relative closure
in
X
with respect to the sharp topology, is called the
support

of
f
. Let
H\Subset_{\text{f}}\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a functionally compact set (see
Def.
37
), we say that
f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GD}\left(H\right)

if
f\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{G}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GC}^{\infty}(\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{C}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{C}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{C}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{C}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{C}})
and
\text{supp}\left(f\right)\subseteq H
.
Such an
f
is called
compactly supported
.

We can now state the uncertainty principle (see
[
58
]
for the
proof):

Theorem 59
.

If
\psi\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GD}(\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
, then
\left(\intop x^{2}\left|\psi\left(x\right)\right|^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\right)\left(\intop\omega^{2}\left|\mathcal{F}\left(\psi\right)\left(\omega\right)\right|^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{4}\|\psi\|_{2}\|\mathcal{F}(\psi)\|_{2}
.

On the contrary with respect the classical formulation in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

of the uncertainty principle, in Thm.
59
we can
e.g. consider
\psi=\delta\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathcal{D}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\mathcal{GD}(\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}})
, and we have

\int x^{2}\delta(x)^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=\left[\int_{-1}^{1}x^{2}b_{\varepsilon}^{2}\mu_{\varepsilon}(b_{\varepsilon}x)^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\right]

where
\mu(x)=[\mu_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})]
is a Colombeau mollifier and

b=[b_{\varepsilon}]\in\mathchoice{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.6}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.56}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.47}{$\rho$}}}{{\vphantom{\mathbb{R}}}{}^{\scalebox{0.35}{$\rho$}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
satisfies
b\geq\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\hbox{\rm d}\rho}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\rho$}}^{-a}
for some

a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
(see embedding Thm.
18
). Since
normalizing the function
\varepsilon\mapsto b_{\varepsilon}^{2}\mu_{\varepsilon}(b_{\varepsilon}x)^{2}

we get an approximate identity, we have
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{-1}^{1}x^{2}b_{\varepsilon}^{2}\mu_{\varepsilon}(b_{\varepsilon}x)^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}=0
,
and hence
\int x^{2}\delta(x)^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\hbox{\rm d}x}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}x$}}\approx 0
is an infinitesimal.
The uncertainty principle Thm.
59
implies that
it is an invertible infinitesimal. Considering the HFT
\mathbb{1}:=\mathcal{F}(\delta)
,
we have

\int\omega^{2}\mathbb{1}(\omega)^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}\geq\int_{-r}^{r}\omega^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}=2\frac{r^{3}}{3}\quad\forall r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

Consequently,
\int\omega^{2}\mathbb{1}(\omega)^{2}\,\mathchoice{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\hbox{\rm d}\omega}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}{\scalebox{0.35}{$\hbox{\rm d}\omega$}}

is an infinite number.

7.
Conclusions

In all the presented examples, the model describes some kind of singular
dynamical system including abrupt changes, impulsive stimuli, nonlinear
discontinuities, infinite barriers, etc. This kind of problems are
ubiquitous in applied mathematics, essentially because the real world
is made of different bodies, having boundaries and frequently interacting
in a non-smooth way. In constructing a model for these systems is
hence important to achieve mathematical simplicity but, at the same
time, a physical reasonably high faithfulness of description.

On the one hand, the use of infinitesimal and infinite numbers has
always been a method to simplify a given problem. Unfortunately, frequently
this technique remains only informal, using “sufficiently small
quantities” or “taking the limit for
\varepsilon\to 0
”, and then
transforming approximated equalities into true ones. As motivational
thoughts, these remain wonderful methods. In our examples, we tried
to show that a corresponding simple and intuitively clear mathematical
theory of these infinitesimal and infinite quantities is possible.
Surprisingly, this theory allows one to arrive at very similar, but
clear and rigorous, thoughts. Therefore, the risk of doing mistakes
is quite lower, and its teaching is also way more clear.

On the other hand, physical systems with singularities are naturally
represented by non-smooth functions. We presented a theory that allows
one to deal with such functions in a way, as if they were smooth,
with a lot of properties that GSF share with ordinary smooth functions.
This is as generalized functions are still informally used in physics
and engineering, despite the fact that Schwartz theory of distributions
is quite old nowadays. Using GSF theory, we can therefore state that
the searched mathematical simplicity in models of singular systems,
possibly with a clear use of infinitesimal or infinite quantities,
is really achievable.
